<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Pharmacogenomics Deep Analysis 50: SLCO1B1 Polymorphisms: OATP1B1 Transporter Defect & Statin Myopathy Susceptibility
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of pharmacogenomics, specifically SLCO1B1 gene polymorphisms affecting OATP1B1 transporter function, in determining statin drug efficacy and risk of myopathy. (理解药代动力学，特别是SLCO1B1基因多态性影响OATP1B1转运蛋白功能，从而决定他汀类药物疗效和肌病风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with a history of hyperlipidemia (LDL-C 160 mg/dL) and hypertension is started on atorvastatin 80 mg daily. He has been taking lisinopril 20 mg daily for hypertension for 5 years without issues. Three weeks after initiating atorvastatin, he presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, progressive proximal muscle weakness, myalgia, and dark urine. His creatine kinase (CK) level is markedly elevated at 15,000 U/L (normal range: 20-200 U/L). Urinalysis shows myoglobinuria. Genetic testing reveals he is homozygous for the SLCO1B1*5 allele. Which of the following mechanisms best explains this patient's adverse reaction to atorvastatin?

(A) Increased hepatic uptake of atorvastatin due to enhanced OATP1B1 transporter activity, leading to higher intracellular concentrations and increased risk of rhabdomyolysis.
(B) Decreased hepatic uptake of atorvastatin due to impaired OATP1B1 transporter function, resulting in increased systemic exposure and accumulation in muscle tissue.
(C) Increased biliary excretion of atorvastatin metabolites due to enhanced OATP1B1 activity, leading to reduced systemic exposure and therapeutic failure.
(D) Decreased renal excretion of atorvastatin due to impaired OATP1B1 function in the proximal tubules, leading to drug accumulation and toxicity.
(E) Increased CYP3A4-mediated metabolism of atorvastatin due to induction by the SLCO1B1*5 allele, resulting in decreased active drug levels and therapeutic failure.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with hyperlipidemia and hypertension.
- **Medication:** Started on high-dose atorvastatin (80 mg).
- **Presentation:** Severe muscle weakness, myalgia, dark urine (suggestive of rhabdomyolysis), markedly elevated CK (15,000 U/L), myoglobinuria. This clinical picture strongly indicates statin-induced myopathy/rhabdomyolysis.
- **Genetic Finding:** Homozygous for SLCO1B1*5 allele. This is the crucial piece of information linking the adverse event to a specific genetic variant.
- **Question:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the adverse reaction.

The key is understanding the function of the OATP1B1 transporter and the effect of the SLCO1B1*5 allele. OATP1B1 (encoded by SLCO1B1) is a hepatic uptake transporter responsible for transporting statins (like atorvastatin) from the blood into hepatocytes. The *5 allele is a loss-of-function allele, meaning it leads to reduced OATP1B1 activity. Reduced hepatic uptake means less statin enters the liver, leading to higher systemic concentrations of the drug. This increased systemic exposure, particularly to muscle tissue, increases the risk of statin-induced myopathy and rhabdomyolysis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **OATP1B1 Function:** Organic Anion Transporting Polypeptide 1B1 is a membrane protein primarily located on the sinusoidal membrane of hepatocytes. It functions as an influx transporter for various organic anions, including statins (atorvastatin, simvastatin, rosuvastatin), bilirubin, and certain drugs.
- **SLCO1B1 Gene & Polymorphisms:** The SLCO1B1 gene encodes the OATP1B1 protein. Common genetic polymorphisms exist, with the *5 allele being the most prevalent loss-of-function variant.
- **Effect of SLCO1B1*5:** Individuals homozygous for the *5 allele (like this patient) have significantly reduced OATP1B1 transporter activity in the liver.
- **Impact on Statin Pharmacokinetics:** Reduced OATP1B1 activity leads to decreased hepatic uptake of statins. This results in:
    - Lower intracellular statin concentrations in hepatocytes (potentially reducing efficacy, although this is less clinically significant than the toxicity risk).
    - Higher plasma concentrations of statins.
    - Increased exposure of peripheral tissues, particularly skeletal muscle, to the statin.
- **Mechanism of Statin Myopathy:** Statins inhibit HMG-CoA reductase, a key enzyme in cholesterol synthesis. While the primary target is the liver, statins also affect muscle cells. High concentrations of statins in muscle can impair mitochondrial function, reduce CoQ10 levels, and interfere with protein synthesis, leading to myalgia, myopathy, and potentially rhabdomyolysis (muscle breakdown releasing CK and myoglobin).
- **Patient Case Link:** The patient's homozygous *5 allele impairs OATP1B1 function, leading to decreased hepatic uptake of atorvastatin. The high dose (80 mg) exacerbates this effect, resulting in elevated systemic atorvastatin levels and increased muscle exposure, culminating in severe myopathy/rhabdomyolysis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. The SLCO1B1*5 allele causes *decreased* OATP1B1 activity (loss-of-function), not enhanced activity. Increased uptake would lead to higher hepatic concentrations, not higher systemic levels and myopathy.
- (C): Incorrect. OATP1B1 is primarily involved in hepatic *uptake*, not biliary excretion. While statins are excreted via bile, OATP1B1's main role is bringing the drug *into* the liver from the blood. Furthermore, reduced OATP1B1 activity would likely decrease, not enhance, biliary excretion of metabolites (as less drug enters the hepatocyte to be metabolized). This option also incorrectly links enhanced activity to reduced systemic exposure.
- (D): Incorrect. OATP1B1 is primarily a hepatic transporter, not a renal transporter. While OATP transporters exist in the kidney, SLCO1B1 is not the primary gene involved in renal statin excretion. Renal excretion is a minor pathway for most statins.
- (E): Incorrect. The SLCO1B1*5 allele affects transporter function, not CYP3A4 enzyme activity. CYP3A4 is the major enzyme responsible for metabolizing atorvastatin. Genetic variations in CYP3A4 can affect statin metabolism, but this is unrelated to the SLCO1B1*5 allele. The *5 allele causes decreased uptake, not increased metabolism.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Correct Mechanism (B)                                  | Distractor (A)                                     | Distractor (C)                                     | Distractor (D)                                     | Distractor (E)                                     |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| **OATP1B1 Activity** | Decreased (due to *5 allele)                          | Increased (Incorrect)                               | Enhanced (Incorrect)                               | Impaired (Incorrect location/transporter)           | Unrelated (CYP3A4)                                 |
| **Hepatic Uptake**   | Decreased                                             | Increased (Incorrect)                               | N/A (Focus on excretion)                            | N/A (Focus on renal)                                | N/A (Focus on metabolism)                            |
| **Systemic Exposure**| Increased                                             | Decreased (Incorrect)                               | Decreased (Incorrect)                               | Increased (Incorrect mechanism)                    | Decreased (Incorrect mechanism)                    |
| **Muscle Exposure**  | Increased                                             | Decreased (Incorrect)                               | N/A                                                | N/A                                                | N/A                                                |
| **Primary Effect**   | Increased risk of myopathy/rhabdomyolysis             | Decreased risk (Incorrect)                          | Therapeutic failure (Incorrect)                    | Toxicity (Incorrect mechanism/location)             | Therapeutic failure (Incorrect mechanism)           |
| **Mechanism**        | Reduced uptake -> High systemic levels -> Muscle toxicity | Enhanced uptake -> High hepatic levels (Incorrect) | Enhanced biliary excretion (Incorrect)             | Decreased renal excretion (Incorrect)              | Increased CYP3A4 metabolism (Incorrect)            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Pharmacogenomics plays a crucial role in drug response and toxicity. The SLCO1B1*5 allele is a common loss-of-function variant that impairs hepatic uptake of statins via the OATP1B1 transporter, increasing systemic exposure and the risk of statin-induced myopathy. Key differentials for statin myopathy include hypothyroidism, vitamin D deficiency, and other drug interactions, but pharmacogenomics provides a specific genetic predisposition.
